The remainder of this entry is about constant-folded arithmetic (`1 // 0`, `"a" * 3` in a position the folder can see), where the same argument — a refusal is a different event from a trap — still applies.<br>`[][0]` and `int("x")` anywhere in a program, even inside a `try` the handler of which would catch it, exit 1 with `gustyc: jit: codegen: list index out of range` / `int on non-integer string`. CPython traps at runtime and the handler runs; the interpreter agrees (`41`). Constant folding has become an evaluation of code the program may never execute, and its failure is reported as a compilation error rather than as the runtime trap the source asks for. Two consequences: a program that *deliberately* provokes and catches such an error cannot be compiled at all, and a refusal appears for a line that would never have run (`if debug: x = [][0]`). The fold must produce an IR-level trap — the same `gy_trap` call the dynamic path emits, with the same class — whenever the operation would raise; the only constants it may resolve are ones the program cannot avoid executing. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-37) |
| Gap R.38 | A refusal message claims something false about the other backend | ⏳ `OPEN` | cli | 0166 | the R.38 table in the record (each row measured on the other leg) | a refusal message claims something false about the other backend (OPEN, diagnostics)<br>The compiled backend refuses some programs by telling you what the interpreter would have done. Most of those sentences are true; one is not. Measured pair by pair:<br>\| refusal emitted by codegen \| its claim \| what the interpreter actually does \| \|------------------------------\|-----------\|-------------------------------------\| \| `operator "%s-format" on a string` — `print("%s" % 2)` \| "the interpreter evaluates it" \| **raises `TypeError`** (exit 3) \| \| `operator "*" on a string` — `print("ab" * 2)` \| "the interpreter evaluates it" \| prints `abab` ✓ true \| \| concatenating a runtime string \| "the interpreter supports it" \| prints `hello world` ✓ true \| \| compiled dict literal with string keys/values \| "the interpreter supports string and other keys" \| true, and the compiled leg supports it too now \| \| sets do not support item assignment \| "the interpreter raises TypeError" \| raises ✓ true \| \| `undefined name` \| "the interpreter reports the same error" \| NameError ✓ true \| \| `len of a non-string variable` — `def n(): return len(xs)` over a module **list** \| (implies the value is a string) \| **prints 2**; the value is a list ✗ — fixed by ADR 0227's `moduleStateErr`, which names module state instead \| \| `string method append on non-constant string` — `xs.append(2)` on a module **list** \| (implies the receiver is a string) \| appends to a list ✗ — same fix, same site \|<br>Both new rows are fixed: a body that reaches module container state is now refused by a message that says what is missing (module-level state, Gap R.35) rather than describing a list as a string. The lesson generalises — a refusal template that asserts something about *another backend* or about an *operand kind* must build its sentence from the gate that knows, and `moduleStateErr` is that gate here. The `undefined name` sentence keeps its claim because it is still checked against a real NameError.<br>The message is one template, `operator %q on a string is not supported in the AOT backend; the interpreter evaluates it`, parameterised by operator — so it asserts the same thing about `%`, which no backend implements, and `*`, which one does. The fix is to stop pasting the claim and derive it: `checkBinOp(op, l, r)` in the interpreter is the single predicate that answers "would this operand pair raise?", so codegen can ask it and say either "the interpreter evaluates it" or "neither backend supports this yet (Gap …)". A refusal is the last thing a stuck program prints, and a sentence about the other path that is true for the operator two lines above and false for this one is how someone ends up trusting an answer that was never available. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-38) |
| Gap R.39 | Reading a name the body also assigns below should be `UnboundLocalError` | ✅ `DONE` | both | 0228 | `unwritten_slot_test.go` | eading a name the body also assigns below should be UnboundLocalError (CLOSED, ADR 0228)<br><code>v = 10<br>def f() -&gt; int:<br>    print(v      # CPython: UnboundLocalError — gusty: prints 10<br>    v = 1<br>    return v</code><br>A consequence of Gap R.35's lenient fallback, recorded rather than glossed: the frame does not know which names are locals before it runs, so the read falls through to the module instead of refusing. **Closed (ADR 0228).** The plumbing was exactly that: `seedLocalsFromBody` pre-records the names a body binds anywhere inside itself, and `Evaluator.bodyBinds` consults it at the read — a name the frame owns is looked for in the frame alone, never in the module, and an unbound one raises `UnboundLocalError` with CPython's own message. The compiled backend raises the same class from the same rule (a written-flag on the slot), so the two backends and CPython now agree on stdout, on class, and on exit 3. Measured before the change: CPython `UnboundLocalError`, both gusty engines `NameError`, and the compiled leg for the sibling shape (`if c: x = 1`) printed `0` and exited 0. `UnboundLocalError` got its own code in the canonical exception table, so `except UnboundLocalError:` matches on both engines (ADR 0212's rule that a built-in trap is a typed raise). | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-39) |
| Gap R.40 | A container slot is a word: ask what fits before writing it | ✅ `DONE` | aot | 0226 | `kind_mismatch_equality.gy`, `container_element_test.go` | a container slot is a word: ask what fits before writing it (CLOSED, ADR 0226)<br><code>print(1 if [1] == [1.0] else 0)   # before: exit 2 -- [1 x i32] [@env_store = internal global ...<br>print([1.5, 2])                    # before: exit 2 -- %t1 = sitofp i32  to double<br>print(1 if 1.0 == [1] else 0)      # before: exit 2 -- %t2 = sitofp i32 @.lst1 to double<br>xs = [1.5]<br>print(xs[0])                       # before: printed 1   (a truncated float, silently)<br>print(1 if {1.5} == {1.6} else 0)  # before: printed 1   (CPython: 0 -- true by truncation)</code><br>Recorded from two repros, measured at six, and the measurement also found three answers that had been green: `{1.0} == {1.0}` compiled to True through the same truncation that made `{1.5} == {1.6}` True. The rule is one question asked in one place — `heapElemKind`, which every container path already goes through, now asks whether the compiled word can hold the element, and refuses with the element kind, the missing representation and the roadmap item that owns it (L11.6).<br>Two mechanism fixes came out of reading the emitted text rather than the error strings:<br>• `emitList` wrote the global's opening text *before* validating elements, so a refusal mid-loop left an unterminated `@.lstN = private global ...` in the module, and every downstream path that caught and ignored that error shipped it. It builds the whole definition, then writes it once.<br>• `valueText` was `v, _ := g.value(b, e); return v` — the third error-swallow this loop has found (after `truthyValue` in ADR 0225 and the method emitter in ADR 0223), and the one that produced `sitofp i32 to double`. Failures now report into the generator and `GenerateIR` refuses: nothing that is about to be refused may also be executed.<br>`1.0 == [1]` is answered by kind (ADR 0215 with ADR 0221's numeric exception), not by coercing a container global through a float conversion.<br>Closed by: `pkg/lang/container_element_test.go` (including the artifact blacklist — `sitofp i32 to double`, `sitofp i32 @.lst`, `[1 x i32] [@`, `ret i32 @.`, `icmp eq i32 @.` — run over the whole family), `integration/container_element_test.go`, parity program `integration/programs/kind_mismatch_equality.gy`. What remains is L11.6 (a float that can live in a slot, which turns each refusal into an answer) and R.37 for the ordering comparisons. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-40) |
| Gap R.41 | A method is a call like any other: own unwind, checked call sites | ✅ `DONE` | aot | 0223 | `method_unwind_test.go`, `method_try.gy` | a method is now a call like any other (CLOSED, ADR 0223)<br>`emitClassMethod` was a code path that had never been brought back to parity with `funcDef`, and three separate wrong interfaces came out of it. Measured before the change, re-checked after:<br>\| shape \| compiled before \| CPython \| \|-------\|-----------------\|---------\| \| `try`/`finally`, `try`/`except`, `break` through a `finally` in a method \| exit 2 — `br label %`, an empty target \| `m fin`/`3`, `handled`/`4`, `fin`/`9` \| \| `raise` out of a method, uncaught \| **prints `0`, exit 0** \| traceback, exit 1 \| \| `raise` out of a method, caught by the caller \| **prints `0`, then the arm** \| the arm alone \| \| nested `self.bad()` that raises \| **prints `0`, the arm never runs** \| the arm \| \| a construct codegen refuses, inside a method \| **exit 2** (half a function emitted) \| — \|<br>The empty label was `g.funcRaiseExit`, set by `funcDef` and never by the method path. The silent `0` was that plus no call-site exception check: the flag was set, and the caller had already taken its value. The dropped `g.stmt` error was the sneakiest — any refused construct inside a method produced a truncated function and an `llc` rejection, exit 2 blaming the compiler for a source error (ADR 0166).<br>Now: the method gets its own `Class_method.raiseexit` block (which closes the root frame it opened, so an unwind does not leak a frame — ADR 0181), its `handlerStack`/`handledArms`/`deferred` are cleared for its body, its traceback frame is named `Class.method`, its first refusal is recorded in `g.emitErr` and returned by `GenerateIR`, and **every** call site into program code checks the flag — static dispatch, `super().m()`, the class-id `switch`, and a constructor's `__init__`. Inside a `switch` arm the check has to finish the arm, so `checkExnLabel` hands back the continuation block and the join's `phi` names *that* as its predecessor: a call site that can raise cannot also be a value producer for the join.<br>Tests: `pkg/lang/method_unwind_test.go` asserts the artifacts — the raise-exit block present, no branch to an empty label, an `@exn_flag` load after a method call, `rt_frame_close` on the unwind path, no branch from inside a method to `main.raiseexit`, `VerifyModuleIR` clean, and a refusal inside a method reported as a compile error — and `integration/method_exceptions_test.go` runs seven shapes on both engines plus the exit-code contract. `programs/method_try.gy` is a standalone parity program, and the debt row for this shape was deleted by the drift test that demands exactly that when a debt is paid. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-41) |
| Gap R.42 | A string value is an `@str_tab` index, not the address of a literal | ✅ `DONE` | aot | 0224 | `string_value_test.go`, `string_values.gy`, `str_loop_eq.gy`, `comp_str_filter.gy` | a string value is an `@str_tab` index, not the address of a literal (CLOSED, ADR 0224)<br><code>class Dog:<br>    def sound(self) -&gt; str:<br>        return "woof"<br>print(Dog().sound())        # interpreter and CPython: woof · compiled: exit 2</code><br>The defect was not the method emitter: it was the **value** path. `value()` returned the string global for a literal, so every shape that reads a string as a value reached `llc` as an `i32` holding an address — `ret i32 @.str1`, `icmp eq i32 @.str1, %t1`, `icmp eq i32 %getresult2, @.str2` — and the compiler took the exit-2 blame for an ordinary program. A measured matrix of 22 string shapes (each expectation taken from CPython, not from the emission) found five such refusals and one silent wrong answer, and none of them was specific to methods.<br>The rule now: a string value is an index into `@str_tab`; the address of a literal appears only where bytes are the question (a `printf` format, an argument to `rt_str_*`, a compile-time fold). Interning a constant emits `call i32 @rt_str_intern2(...)`; text the runtime creates itself is interned there; printing an index reads it back with `rt_str_ptr`; folds that *produce* strings intern their result. Element-kind facts follow the value: `exprIsString` answers the static question for a comprehension element so `listElemStr` can be recorded, and an `-> str` method registers under its mangled symbol so its callers know the `i32` they hold means text.<br>Two things underneath had to give way at the same time. The static-dispatch emitter began the `call` line before evaluating arguments, so an argument needing an instruction of its own was written into the middle of the operand list; and the filtered comprehension's loop header declared the *body* as its back edge when the increments came from the *skip* block, which is the second bug the L11.8 refusal had been covering (`PHI node entries do not match predecessors!`). Both fixed, with the `phi` asserted to name the block that actually branches to it.<br>Closed by: `pkg/lang/string_value_test.go`, `TestStringElementFilterCompiles`, `integration/string_value_test.go`, parity programs `string_values.gy`, `str_loop_eq.gy` and `comp_str_filter.gy` (the last two are promoted probes; the matrix no longer carries their rows). What remains is recorded as R.46 and R.45 rather than left implicit. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-42) |
| Gap R.45 | A subscript of a string is a one-character string | ✅ `DONE` | both | 0225 | `string_subscript_test.go`, `string_subscript.gy` | a subscript of a string is a one-character string (CLOSED, ADR 0225)<br><code>s = "abc"<br>print(s[1])             # CPython: b · both backends before: 98<br>print(s[0] + s[2])      # CPython: ac · both backends before: 196<br>print(1 if s[1] == "b" else 0)   # CPython: 1 · both backends before: 0</code><br>Recorded from the interpreter side, but the compiled fold had the same missing type and produced the same wrong values, and nothing in the suite noticed because the two backends agreed with each other — only the CPython leg can see that `98` is not `b`. Sixteen measured shapes, zero matching before. The character is a string now, allocated in the interpreter and interned in codegen, and the unit it agrees on is the code point: `len("café")` is 4 (was 5), `"café"[3]` is `é`, `s[2:]` cannot cut a character in half, `ord` takes the first rune.<br>What the measurement uncovered on the way was worse than what we came for: `truthyValue` returned `asI1(b, "0")` when a condition failed to lower, believing "the enclosing statement path still has the error" — the ternary path had no error channel, so `print(1 if s[1] == "b" else 0)` emitted `icmp ne i32 0, 0` and printed `0`. A part of a program that cannot be lowered is a compile error, never a default value (asserted in `TestUnlowerableConditionIsAnErrorNotAFalseBranch`).<br>Closed by: `pkg/lang/string_subscript_test.go`, `integration/string_subscript_test.go`, parity program `integration/programs/string_subscript.gy`. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-45) |
| Gap R.46 | Printing an element of a freshly built comprehension list prints its index | ✅ `DONE` — closed 2026-10-02 with ADR 0244: the comprehension's list-binding now asks the element's kind of the **iterated object** (`compElemPrintsAsText`), which is still in scope after the loop closes, instead of of the loop variable's own facts, which are not. A run-time-grown text list keeps its loop variable's text kind, so `print(out)` and `print(out[0])` give one answer; the row that was pinned at the wrong output is now a parity case | aot | 0187, 0241, 0244 | `TestPrintingAnElementOfAFreshComprehensionListMatchesCPython` (`integration/string_value_test.go`), `TestComprehensionOverAOneKindContainerTagsItsElement` (`pkg/lang/comprehension_brace_element_test.go`), `TestComprehensionOverAOneKindContainerMatchesCPython` (`integration/comprehension_brace_element_test.go`) | printing an element of a freshly built comprehension list prints its index (OPEN, compiled)<br><code>names = ["a", "b"]<br>names.append("c")<br>out = [n for n in names if n == "a"]<br>print(out[0])           # CPython: a · compiled: 0</code><br>`0` is the interned index of `"a"` printed with `%d`. The list knows its elements are strings once something has walked it — the same program with a preceding `for n in names:` loop prints `a`, because that loop is what registers the element kind under the shared variable name. A parity program that happened to have such a loop is exactly the kind of test that goes green for the wrong reason, so it now asserts only what the oracle agrees with, and this shape is pinned at what the compiler does (`TestPrintingAnElementOfAFreshComprehensionListIsPinned`). Fix: have the comprehension's list-binding ask `exprIsString` of the element *and* of the iterated name, and have the element printer fall back to the same facts the container printer uses. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-46) |
| Gap R.47 | A compiled string is a compile-time value only (no run-time reads, no run-time construction) | ✅ `DONE` — no new representation: reads and writes go through the interned table | aot | 0229, 0230 | `runtime_string_ops.gy`, `runtime_string_writes.gy` | a compiled string is a compile-time value only (CLOSED, ADR 0229 + ADR 0230)<br><code>s = "abc"<br>i = 0<br>print(s[i])          # interpreter and CPython: a · compiled: refuses<br>print(len(s[1]))     # interpreter and CPython: 1 · compiled: refuses<br>print(s[1].upper())  # interpreter and CPython: B · compiled: refuses<br>print(ord(s[1]))     # interpreter and CPython: 98 · compiled: refuses<br>for c in s:          # interpreter and CPython: a, b, c · compiled: refuses<br>    print(c)</code><br>**Closed — no new representation was needed** (ADR 0229 reads, ADR 0230 writes). A compiled string value is already an `@str_tab` index (ADR 0224) and `rt_str_intern` already appends by content, so the table grows while the program runs: the operations take indices and return indices, and print, equality, substring tests and container slots keep working on a string the compiler never saw. Ten runtime helpers in all — `rt_str_from_bytes`, `rt_str_nchars`, `rt_str_char`, `rt_str_codepoint`, `rt_str_case`, `rt_str_cat`, `rt_str_byteoff`, `rt_str_slice`, `rt_str_strip`, `rt_str_of_int` — plus a `for`-over-string that drives the ordinary counter loop with `rt_str_nchars`/`rt_str_char`.<br>What was actually missing was a *question*: each operation asked "can the compiler read this string's text?" where the question is "is this a string?", which is why `"abc"[1]` answered and `get()[1]` refused, why `s[1].lower()` printed text while `s[1].upper()` printed `2`, and why a correct slice printed `1`. The whole-program kind scan (`scanStringBindings`) and the two classification predicates now answer once — and adding an operation means adding its case to operations, print and the scan in the same commit.<br>Both halves are covered against CPython: `programs/runtime_string_ops.gy` (reads) and `programs/runtime_string_writes.gy` (writes) are matrix rows declared `match`, and `TestCompiledStringSubscriptAnswersAtRuntime` / `TestCompiledStringWritesAnswerAtRuntime` refuse to run if CPython disagrees with the table. Iterating a runtime string is the shape that had been *silently* wrong rather than refused — the table index read as a repeat count, printing nothing with exit 0 (Gap R.16) — so those tests assert output, not acceptance.<br>Not claimed by the closure, and written down rather than half-implemented: compiled case folding is ASCII only and `strip` trims the ASCII whitespace set (the interpreter has both full tables); `str(<float>)` refuses rather than truncate a float it cannot yet hold (L11.6); `%s` formatting is Gap R.31; `str * int` and list concatenation are Gap R.33; the table's 4096-entry bound raises a catchable `RuntimeError` (ADR 0229).<br>Process finding worth keeping: `for c in txt()` had quietly become the canonical "the compiled backend refuses" fixture in four tests — the exit-code contract, the trap-class contract, the oracle's refused-leg check and a diagnostics table. When the shape became answerable they went green while exercising nothing. Four fixtures moved to `print("ab" * 2)` (still refused) and the pinned refusal tables were emptied into CPython-checked answer tables. A pinned refusal is a claim about the future: when the gap closes, the pin has to move. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-47) |
| Gap R.48 | There is no `global` statement (all three engines differ) | ⏳ `PLANNED` | both | 0227, 0228 | `probe_global_statement` | there is no `global` statement (OPEN, language surface)<br><code>def touch():<br>    global gz<br>    if 0:<br>        gz = 1<br>    return gz<br>print(touch())</code><br>CPython reads `global gz` as a declaration and the read raises `NameError: name 'gz' is not defined`. gusty has no such statement, so the line parses as the *expression* `global gz`: the interpreter reports `name 'global' is not defined`, and the compiled backend refuses the program. Three engines, three answers, on a construct every Python reader will reach for — and the worst property of the three is that two of them are wrong in ways no one reading the file would predict.<br>Pinned as `programs/probe_global_statement.gy` (an oracle row with both legs' behaviour recorded), found during ADR 0228's probe pass and left out of that cycle rather than folded in, because the fix is the statement itself: a declaration that routes reads and writes of the named entries to module state — which is exactly the `@gy_mod_<name>` storage ADR 0227 already built for rebound module scalars, so the machinery is there and only the surface is missing. The checker side needs the mirror rule of ADR 0228's `seedLocalsFromBody`: a `global` name is *not* a local of the frame, whatever the body assigns. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-48) |
| Gap R.49 | A module variable whose name was also a parameter of an earlier `def` gets no slot: `store i32 %t5, i32* %_x` → `llc` rejects the module (exit 2, an ordinary program blamed on the compiler); renaming either binding compiles | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 sweep | aot | 0163, 0211, 0228 | measured repro in the record | code>def f(x): return x * 2<br>x = 8<br>x /= 2<br>print(x)</code><br>The interpreter prints `4.0`, CPython prints `4.0`, and the compiled backend does not build: codegen emits `store i32 %t5, i32* %_x`, and `llc-20` rejects the module with "use of undefined value '%_x'" — an ordinary program leaving the compiler as exit **2**, a compiler bug under the exit-code contract (ADR 0211). Renaming either binding (`def g(q)`, or the module name) compiles, so the fault is name-keyed: the module-level `x` is treated as already bound because a *parameter* of that name exists in an earlier body. It is the same disease as Gap I.3's leaked per-body state (ADR 0163) and R.36's slot bookkeeping (ADR 0228): a variable's slot is allocated once per call, and a name that some other scope used must not count as an allocation the module can reuse. Fix with the binding-scope rules that L11.1's tagged slots land on, and pin it with a parity program plus an IR-shape test that the module allocas are hoisted and per-scope. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-49) |
| Gap R.50 | `round` ties away from zero where CPython ties to even (`round(2.5)` = 3, Python 2) — identical on both backends, so parity cannot see it | ✅ `DONE` — closed 2026-10-02 (ADR 0236); the `ndigits` half found while closing it is Gap R.69 | both | 0104 | measured repro in the record (needs an oracle row, not a parity row) | <code>print(round(2.5))   # CPython 2   gusty --interp 3   gusty --aot 3<br>print(round(3.5))   # CPython 4   gusty --interp 4   gusty --aot 4<br>print(round(-0.5))  # CPython 0   gusty --interp -1  gusty --aot -1<br>print(round(0.5))   # CPython 0   gusty --interp 1   gusty --aot 1</code><br>Both backends agree with each other and disagree with Python, which is exactly the class of defect the two-backend parity matrix can never see: `round` rounds halves away from zero where CPython rounds to the nearest *even* value. The oracle leg of the conformance matrix is the only instrument that can find it, and the fix belongs with L11.6 — the same cycle that settles numeric typing — because `round` also has to keep its result type (`round(2.5)` is an `int` in Python). The pin should be an oracle row, not a parity row, so that the fix is what closes it.<br>• ✅ **Done (ADR 0236): ties go to the nearest EVEN value.** Four places said the wrong rule, not one: `math.Round` in the evaluator, `@llvm.round.f64` in the compiled runtime, `math.Round` again in the compiled *constant fold*, and four tests — two of which stated it in prose (`must round half-away in the AOT binary`) and one of which pinned the string `i32 3` in the IR. Both backends now name the IEEE operation instead of implementing a rule: `math.RoundToEven`, `call double @llvm.roundeven.f64`. `integration/programs/round_ties.gy` entered the corpus with **no ledger row** — which here means "print what CPython prints", on both legs — covering literal ties (the fold) and variable ties (the intrinsic) separately, because they are separate paths and fixing one leaves the other wrong. Matrix row 104, `oracle: match` both legs: the old answer now fails CI. The four inverted pins keep their old values in a comment, because "the prose, the interpreter and the compiler all said the same wrong thing, and CPython was not in the room" is the lesson worth carrying. `docs/language.md`'s builtin paragraph was rewritten out of the same pile — it claimed `round(x)` truncates *and* folds-and-is-a-no-op *and* that "the AOT backend has no float representation", in one sentence. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-50) |
| Gap R.51 | **`floor` / `ceil` / `sqrt`: the checker predeclared them, the interpreter trapped `NameError` (exit 3), and the compiler answered with a float where Python returns an `int`** | ✅ `DONE` — closed 2026-10-03 (ADR 0264); what the closure measured is now Gaps R.133, R.134, R.135 and R.136 | both | 0264 | `pkg/lang/math_names.go` + `pkg/lang/math_names_test.go` (the 30-shape value table on both engines against the reference's `math.*` twin, the trap half with its catchability, the word boundary walked from both sides, the IR rows, the arity half, the filed-not-fixed table with a number per engine), `integration/math_names_test.go` (the same through the shipped CLI against `python3` with `from math import floor, ceil, sqrt` on the first line — same bytes, one line apart — exit classes pinned, exit 2 failing the file), `programs/whole_number_builtins.gy` and `programs/non_finite_float_constant.gy` in `conformance_cases.go` — `not_applicable` for the spelling only, twin-asserted in the CLI test | `print(floor(3.7))` was `3` in CPython, `NameError`/exit 3 interpreted and `3.0` compiled; `print(ceil(-0.5))` was `0`, `NameError`, `-0.0`; `print(sqrt(-1))` was a `ValueError`, `NameError`, and **`nan` with exit 0**; `print(floor("a"))` was a `TypeError`, `NameError`, and `0.0`. One name, three engines, three behaviours, and the checker in the middle calling all of them well-typed — with `TestExecFloorCeil` pinning `2.0\n-3.0\n3.0\n-2.0` under a comment quoting the language's own "the largest double <=", and roadmap's own Gap P.2 quoting the same phrase back. Three voices agreeing with each other and with nothing outside.<br>What shipped is one rule per name in one file, read by both backends: `floor`/`ceil` answer a **whole number** (the reference's `math.floor`/`math.ceil` return an `int`), `sqrt` answers a float; the checker types them the same way (`TInt()`/`TFlt()`), because a name the checker types one way and the codegen lowers another is how this stayed invisible. A whole number beside a float is **widened, never truncated** (ADR 0243's (payload, widen) step): `floor(2.7) + 1.5` is `3.5`, `[floor(2.7), ceil(2.2)]` is `[2, 3]`, `str(floor(2.7))` is `2`. Every bad argument is a **raise the reference wrote**, exit 3 and catchable by class on both engines — `must be real number, not str` (unquoted, unlike this language's other `TypeError` family), `math domain error`, `cannot convert float NaN to integer`, `cannot convert float infinity to integer` — with `OverflowError` added to the exception table (tag 9) so `except OverflowError:` means one thing on all three. `sqrt(-1)` is a run-time raise, not a compile-time refusal, so `try: sqrt(n) except ValueError:` stays writable. Arity is one sentence per name, exit 1 compiled / exit 3 interpreted, exit 2 nowhere.<br>29 of the 30 value shapes print CPython's bytes on both engines; the 30th is the already-filed loop-variable wall (Gap R.130), pinned and not asserted. What the sweep found on the way is filed with its own ID rather than absorbed here: the compiled `int` word's edge (R.133), a non-finite constant that could not be emitted at all — **exit 2** on `x = float("inf")` (R.134, fixed in this commit because `sqrt` needed it), exponent literals that do not lex (R.135), and the ten table names no engine can call (R.136). | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-51) |
| Gap R.52 | stdlib discovery walks up from the **cwd**, so an installed `gustyc` cannot `import math` unless `--stdlib` / `$GUSTY_STDLIB_DIR` is set | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 sweep; owner: L9.2 (the install story) | cli | — | `import math` fails outside a directory under a repo with `stdlib/`, succeeds inside one | `pkg/lang/resolve.go` walks up from the **current working directory** looking for a `stdlib` directory, so this works from inside a checkout and fails anywhere else:<br><code>$ cd /home/donutloop/Workspace/gusty &amp;&amp; gustyc --interp /tmp/pp/m1.gy   # 3.141592653589793<br>$ cd /tmp/pp                    &amp;&amp; gustyc --interp m1.gy                # cannot import module math, exit 3</code><br>The escape hatches exist and are documented (`--stdlib <dir>`, `$GUSTY_STDLIB_DIR`, and `docs/language.md` § On-disk stdlib modules), which is why nobody has tripped over them yet: the development workflow always runs from the repo. An *installed* binary has no repo above it, so the first thing a user does — `pyre hello.gy` with `import math` in it — traps. The fix is one more entry in the search list: beside the executable, and beside `argv[0]`'s `../share/pyre/stdlib`, with the root actually searched reported in `--json` (a machine should be able to ask which stdlib answered). It belongs with L9.2, because a package manager is the moment the binary leaves the repo. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-52) |
| Gap R.53 | Comparison chains answer the wrong value on **both** backends (`1 < x < 3` takes the branch) | ✅ `DONE` — closed by ADR 0288 on 2026-07-06, the same change that closed L12.1; the chain is one node now, so the boolean-against-int comparison the grammar used to build does not exist | both | — | `programs/probe_comparison_chains.gy` — `3 < 2 < 1`, `1 < 2 > 1`, `1 > 2 < 3` and `1 < 2 < 3 < 1` each answered the opposite verdict at exit 0 on BOTH engines; `print(1 < 2 < 3)` was the accidental pass | comparison chains answer the wrong value (OPEN, both backends)<br><code>x = 5<br>if 1 &lt; x &lt; 3:<br>    print("in")<br>else:<br>    print("out")      # CPython: out — both backends: in<br>print(1 &lt; 2 &lt; 3)    # True — right by accident<br>print(10 &lt; x &lt; 20)  # CPython: False — both backends: 1</code><br>`a &lt; b &lt; c` is parsed as `(a &lt; b) &lt; c`: the middle operand ends up compared against a boolean, so the chain is not "two comparisons" but "one comparison and a nonsense second one". `1 &lt; 2 &lt; 3` is correct only because `(True) &lt; 3` happens to agree with Python, which is the most dangerous kind of pass — a test on that one line would certify the operator. Parity cannot see it (both legs agree); only the oracle leg can. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-53) |
| Gap R.54 | The iterator protocol: `for` over `__iter__`/`__next__` spins forever in the interpreter and iterates nothing in the compiler | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.2 | both | — | measured repro in the record (interpreter produced 7.3 M lines in 15 s before the harness killed it) | the iterator protocol is not a protocol (OPEN, both backends)<br><code>class R:<br>    def __init__(self): self.i = 0<br>    def __iter__(self): return self<br>    def __next__(self):<br>        self.i = self.i + 1<br>        if self.i &gt; 2: raise StopIteration<br>        return self.i<br>for x in R():<br>    print(x)<br>print("after")      # CPython: 1 2 after</code><br>The interpreter calls `__next__`, receives the `StopIteration`, and does not treat it as the end signal — it printed **7.3 million integers in 15 s** before the harness timed it out. The compiled backend prints `after` and exits **0**: zero iterations, no diagnostic, for an iterable with elements. Two engines, two different wrong answers, and a CI that runs both would call the program green on neither. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-54) |
| Gap R.55 | `print` ignores `__str__` / `__repr__`: an instance prints `<instance>` interpreted and `0` compiled | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.3 | both | — | measured repro in the record | print does not consult the class (OPEN, both backends)<br><code>class C:<br>    def __str__(self): return "S"<br>    def __repr__(self): return "R"<br>print(C())          # CPython: S · --interp: &lt;instance&gt; · --aot: 0</code><br>The compiled leg is the worse one: `0` is an interned-string index rendered with `%d` — Gap R.22's signature arriving through the printer instead of a return. `repr(C())` is not a built-in at all (`NameError: name 'repr' is not defined`), so the method that containers, tracebacks and debuggers render through does not exist; `__str__` is defined by the program and called by nothing. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-55) |
| Gap R.56 | A class body binds no attributes: `class C: X = 1` then `C.X` raises `AttributeError` | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.4 | both | — | measured repro in the record | class-level attributes do not exist (OPEN, both backends)<br><code>class C:<br>    X = 1<br>print(C.X)              # CPython: 1<br>print(C().get())        # a method reading self.X: same AttributeError</code><br>interpreter `AttributeError: type object 'C' has no attribute 'X'`; codegen `codegen: unsupported attr expression`. Only methods land in the class object, so the whole family of idioms that bind *values* in a body is unwritable: class constants, counters, registries, and `class Color(Enum): RED = 1` — which is why there is no `enum` to import rather than a half-built one. It is one missing write in the class-body lowering, and it is the cheapest row in this phase. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-56) |
| Gap R.57 | `@property` yields the method object; `@staticmethod` / `@classmethod` do not compile | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.5 | both | — | measured repro in the record | the descriptor built-ins are not built-ins (OPEN)<br><code>class T:<br>    def __init__(self, v): self._v = v<br>    @property<br>    def d(self): return self._v * 2<br>print(T(3).d)              # CPython: 6 · --interp: &lt;method&gt; · --aot: refusal</code><br><code>class S:<br>    @staticmethod<br>    def s(): return 7<br>    @classmethod<br>    def c(cls): return 8<br>print(S.s(), S.c())        # CPython: 7 8 · --interp: 7 8 · --aot: refusal</code><br>Decorator *application* is real in the interpreter (an AOT limit the tracker records), so the gap is specifically that `property`, `staticmethod` and `classmethod` are absent from the built-in table: the first silently means nothing, the second pair are interpreter-only. `@property` printing `<method>` is a silent wrong answer, not an absence. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-57) |
| Gap R.58 | The call surface: `*args`, `**kwargs`, keyword-only parameters and call-site unpacking do not parse | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.6 | both | — | measured repro in the record | the star half of the call surface does not parse (OPEN)<br><code>def f(a, *rest, k=1, **kw): …      # parse error at 1:10: expected identifier<br>def g(a, b=2, *, c=3): …           # parse error at 1:15: expected identifier<br>g(*[1, 2])                         # parse error at 4:9: unexpected token<br>g(**{"a": 1, "b": 2})              # parse error: unexpected token</code><br>CPython runs all four. Keyword *arguments* already work (`print(…, sep=, end=)`, Gap J.1), so the parser is the boundary: nothing behind it — checker arity, the interpreter's binding, codegen's argument lowering — has ever seen a packed parameter or an unpacked call. The pack target is a tuple, so L11.3 is the natural dependency rather than a blocker on the parameter-list half. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-58) |
| Gap R.59 | `assert`, `del`, `nonlocal` are identifiers, not keywords; `...` is not a body | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.7 (the `global` half is Gap R.48) | both | — | measured repro in the record | four statements that are really names (OPEN, diagnostics + surface)<br><code>assert x &gt; 0, "must be positive"   # --check: error at 1:1: undefined name "assert"<br>                                   # --file: NameError at run time, exit 3<br>del d["a"]                         # NameError: name 'del' is not defined<br>nonlocal n                          # same shape inside a nested def<br>def f():<br>    ...                            # parse error at 2:5: unexpected token</code><br>The checker catches it and the runner does not: the same source is a front-end error under `--check` and a runtime trap under `--file`, which is the split this loop has already decided is unacceptable (ADR 0211's one-event-one-code, extended: an unimplemented statement is a *compile*-time fact). `...` as a body is the one that hurts most, because it is how a `Protocol`, an abstract method and a stub are written. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-59) |
| Gap R.60 | f-string conversions and format specs are parsed away; `str.format` does not exist | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.8 | both | — | measured repro in the record | f-strings interpolate but do not format (OPEN, both backends)<br><code>x = 3.5<br>print(f"{x:.2f}")   # CPython: 3.50 · both backends: 3.5<br>print(f"{x!r}")     # CPython: 3.5 via repr · both: 3.5 via str<br>print(f"{x:&gt;6}")    # CPython: '    3.5' · both backends: 3.5<br>print("{},{}".format("x", "y"))   # no such string method</code><br>Plain interpolation is correct on all three engines (`f"hello {name} {1 + 2}"` → `hello world 3`), so the missing half is precise rather than vague: the `!conversion` and `:spec` fields are dropped at parse time and the formatter is not there to apply them. Silently printing a narrower rendering than asked for is a wrong answer with a plausible face — the class of defect only the oracle leg finds. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-60) |
| Gap R.61 | Match patterns: star patterns, `case T() as x` and keyword class patterns do not parse, and a dict binding prints `(null)` | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.9 (with Gap B) | both | — | measured repro in the record | four pattern shapes, three refusals and one wrong answer (OPEN)<br><code>case [first, *rest]:          # parse error at 3:22: unexpected token<br>case str() as s:              # parse error at 3:20: expected ':'<br>case Point(x=0, y=0):         # parse error at 7:21: expected ')' in class pattern<br>def f(d):<br>    match d:<br>        case {"k": v}: return v<br>print(f({"k": 7}))            # CPython: 7 · --interp: 7 · --aot: (null)</code><br>The three parse refusals are a grammar gap in front of work the matcher already does (guards, or-patterns, list/dict/class patterns all lower). The fourth is not: `(null)` is the mixed-type/untagged-value signature (Gap R.22, Phase 11's tag) arriving through a pattern binding, so this row splits — grammar now, print when L11.1 lands. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-61) |
| Gap R.62 | `from … import … [as …]` does not parse, and `__name__` is undefined so the entry-point idiom is unwritable | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.10 | both | — | measured repro in the record | imports stop at `import name`, and a module does not know its own name (OPEN)<br><code>from math import sqrt              # parse error at 1:1: unexpected token<br>from math import sqrt as r         # same<br>print(__name__)                    # NameError: name '__name__' is not defined<br>if __name__ == "__main__":         # so this line cannot be written<br>    main()</code><br>`import math` itself resolves (subject to the cwd-relative discovery of Gap R.52) and `math.PI` folds, but `math.sqrt` reports `no name sqrt in module` because the bundled stdlib is four data-only modules — that half belongs to the stdlib item, this row is the grammar and the module's self-knowledge. `__name__` / `__main__` are what turn a file into a program: every script in the world's most-used language starts with this line. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-62) |
| Gap R.63 | A list or dict method name is answered from the **string** method table; the built-in list is short | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.11 | both | — | measured repro in the record | method dispatch is keyed on the name instead of the receiver (OPEN)<br><code>xs = [1, 2]<br>xs.insert(0, 0)      # --interp: no such list method · --aot: codegen: string method insert on non-constant string<br>xs.index(3)          # same shape<br>d.setdefault("b", 2) # --aot: string method setdefault on non-constant string<br>map(f, xs) · filter(f, xs) · getattr(o, "v") · repr(x) · hash(x) · id(x) · vars(o) · dir(o)   # NameError or a codegen refusal<br>frozenset([1, 2]) · tuple([1, 2]) · b"abc"                                                        # absent<br>{1, 2} &amp; {2, 3}                                                                                   # parse error</code><br>The compiled backend looks a method up in the string table for any receiver whose kind it cannot see, so a **list** receives a message about strings — Gap R.38's rule (never assert something about another backend or operand kind from a template) broken structurally rather than by a pasted sentence. `xs.sort()`, `xs.index/count/remove`, `dict.setdefault/pop`, `str.format`, `map`/`filter`, the reflection built-ins, `frozenset`, `tuple(…)` and bytes literals are simply not there on either path. Fix: one table per receiver kind, consulted by all three consumers — `predeclared.go`'s single-source rule, applied to methods. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-63) |
| Gap R.64 | `int` overflows silently, and the two backends overflow differently (`2 ** 63` is `-9.2e18`, `0`, and `9.2e18`) | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.12 | both | — | measured repro in the record | integers wrap in silence, and the legs disagree (OPEN)<br><code>print(2 ** 62)   # 4611686018427387904 — exact on both backends<br>print(2 ** 63)   # CPython 9223372036854775808 · --interp -9223372036854775808 · --aot 0<br>print(10 ** 19)  # CPython 10000000000000000000 · --interp -8446744073709551616 · --aot -1981284352<br>print(10 ** 30)  # CPython 1e30 · --interp 5076944270305263616 · --aot 1073741824</code><br>Three different answers for one program, none of them CPython's, none of them reported. The compiled path truncates to `i32` where the interpreter keeps an `i64`, so the divergence is two layers deep: width, then representation. Parity cannot see it because the legs disagree — this needs an oracle row plus an ADR that decides whether gusty has bignums (a real 2026 choice, made once) or a documented bounded `int` that **traps** on overflow. What is not defensible is the status quo: a number that becomes a different number, quietly, in arithmetic a program can see coming. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-64) |
| Gap R.65 | `gustyc --lang` under-reports the surface that ships, so the machine-readable spec is stale | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.13 | tooling | — | `gustyc --lang` against `docs/language.md` and the census in the record | the surface manifest is hand-written and stale (OPEN)<br>`--lang` prints `statements: assign, print, if/elif/else, while, for-in-range, def/return, pass, match, try/except/finally, raise, class, import`. It omits `with` — which ships on **both** backends and has a section in `docs/language.md` — plus `async def`, `yield`, `yield from`, `break` and `continue`; the expression line omits `in`, `is`, `**`, the ternary, the walrus and f-strings, all of which run. The command is honest in direction (it never claims a construct that does not exist, which is the dangerous direction) and stale in the safe direction, which is exactly how a manifest rots. The audience decides the fix: AGENTS.md makes self-description part of every feature, so the manifest is generated from the tables the lexer, parser, checker and codegen already consult, per construct per backend, and the three-engine census is the check that it cannot drift again. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-65) |
| Gap R.66 | The interpreter's attribute lookup stops at the first base: `class C(A, B)` resolves `A`'s methods and not `B`'s — while the compiled backend resolves both | ⏳ `OPEN` — found by the 2026-10-01 surface survey; owner: L12.4 | interp | — | measured repro in the record | the interpreter walks one base (OPEN, the rare AOT-correct inversion)<br><code>class A:<br>    def a(self): return "a"<br>class B:<br>    def b(self): return "b"<br>class C(A, B):<br>    pass<br>print(C().a())     # a on every engine<br>print(C().b())     # CPython: b · --aot: b · --interp: AttributeError: 'C' has no attribute 'b'</code><br>The compiled backend walks every base and answers correctly; the interpreter walks only the first and reports a missing attribute for a method the program inherited. Two MRO implementations, one right, is the argument for the table: the linearisation belongs in one place both legs consult, next to the class attributes of Gap R.56 that it would also resolve through. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-66) |
| Gap R.67 | A container returned from a function is not a value in the compiled backend: `return [1, 2]` emits `ret i32 @.lst1` (exit 2), and `la = [1, 2]; return la` compiles and prints `0` | ⏳ `OPEN` — found 2026-10-02 while closing Gap J.2; owner: L11.1 (the tag) | aot | — | measured on three engines, repro in the record | a returned container is not a value (OPEN)<br><code>def f():<br>    return [1, 2]</code> prints <code>[1, 2]</code> interpreted and in CPython; compiled it is exit 2 — <code>ret i32 @.lst1</code>, a global in the i32 return slot, which <code>llc</code> rejects. <code>def f():<br>    la = [1, 2]<br>    return la</code> compiles on all three and prints <code>0</code> compiled, where the interpreter and CPython print <code>[1, 2]</code> — the handle, printed as the number it is. Sets (<code>ret i32 @.set1</code>) and dicts (<code>ret i32 @.dict1</code>) behave the same, so this is the ADR 0163 binding rule asked one statement further out: the <code>return</code> needs a heap handle and the function needs a recorded return kind for <code>print(f())</code> to reach the container printer. Found while closing Gap J.2 — the comprehension binding no longer refuses first, so it now reaches this. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-67)
| Gap R.68 | A `match` capture bound only by an arm that did not run is readable in the compiled backend | ⏳ `OPEN` — found 2026-10-02 while closing Gap B; owner: L11.8 (the refusal model) with ADR 0228's definite-assignment rule | both | — | measured on three engines, repro in the record | A capture a skipped arm would have bound (OPEN)<br><code>def probe(v):<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;match v:<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;case Point(x):<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;print("bound", x)<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;case 5:<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;print("five", x)<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;return 0<br>probe(Point(1, 2))<br>probe(5)</code> prints `bound 1` then raises `NameError: name 'x' is not defined` (exit 3) in the interpreter, and prints `bound 1` then `five 0` compiled — the capture's slot, which nothing on that path wrote. `UnwrittenReads` (ADR 0228) already asks the definite-assignment question of a function body's locals; a `match` arm's captures are not yet edges in that graph, so codegen emits the load and the answer is whatever the alloca holds. The fix is that graph, not a new analysis, and the refusal must name the arm. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-68)
| Gap R.69 | **`round(x, ndigits)` had three behaviours, one of them the wrong exit code**: `print(round(2.345, 2))` was `2.35` in CPython, `2` in the interpreter (the digit count was never read) and an exit-1 refusal compiled (`round expects one argument`) — exit 1, the contract's *your program is wrong* class, on a program the reference runs; `round(3.5, 0)` was `4.0`/`4`/the same refusal, and `round()` was a Go panic in the evaluator (**exit 2**) | ✅ `DONE` — closed 2026-10-03 (ADR 0263); the four shapes found on the way are Gaps R.129–R.132 | both | 0263 | `pkg/lang/round_digits.go` + `pkg/lang/round_digits_test.go` (parity table × both engines against CPython, the IR rows, the trap half with its catchability, the arity half, and the rule on its own with the swept values and both clamps), `integration/round_digits_test.go` (the same through the shipped CLI against `python3`, exit classes pinned, exit 2 failing the file), `programs/round_ndigits.gy` in `conformanceStandalone()` — `oracle: match`, parity yes | The row's own suggested algorithm — scale by `10**n`, ask the named ties-to-even operation, unscale — was **built and rejected by measurement**: it prints CPython's `2.35` for the row's own DoD example and differs from the reference on 1,077 of 375,224 fractional pairs and 74,838 of 156,048 negative-digit ones, because scaling manufactures ties the value does not have (`0.005` is 0.00500000000000000010408… and rounds up to `0.01`; ×100 it is exactly `0.5`, which a nearest-even rule answers `0`). What the reference rounds is the *exact decimal value of the double*, so both backends borrow a correctly-rounded double→decimal conversion instead of writing one — Go's `strconv.FormatFloat`/`ParseFloat` in the evaluator, `snprintf("%.*f")`/`strtod` in `@rt_round_digits` — swept 531,272 pairs against CPython, residual 143 all at \|x\| between 4.117e18 and 1e300 and one ULP, filed rather than tested around. The answer is the kind the value arrived as (`round(3.5, 0)` is `4.0`, `round(5, 2)` is `5`, `round(True, 2)` is `1`); a non-integer digit count is **raised** as CPython's `TypeError` and catchable on both engines (exit 3, not exit 1); the arity sentence is written once and read by both backends (exit 1 compiled, 3 interpreted, 2 nowhere)<br>`print(round(2.345, 2))   # CPython 2.35 · --interp 2.35 · --aot 2.35`<br>`print(round(3.5, 0))     # CPython 4.0  · --interp 4.0  · --aot 4.0` | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-69) |
| Gap R.70 | `for` over a **set or dict literal** falls through to the range path: `for v in {1, 2}` emitted `icmp slt i32 %_ctr1, @.set1` (exit 2) and bound the loop variable to the counter | ✅ `DONE` — found 2026-10-02 while measuring nested containers; fixed with ADR 0239 | both | 0239 | `integration/for_container_literal_test.go` — three engines, CPython the oracle, incl. a mixed set compared as a multiset | `for v in {1, 2}: print(v)` (CLOSED)<br><code>for v in {1, 2}:<br>    print(v)          # was: exit 2 — a global in an i32 slot; now 1, 2 on every engine<br>for k in {"a": 1, "b": 2}:<br>    print(k)      # was: exit 2, or the counter; now a, b — a dict yields its KEYS<br>for v in {1, "a"}:<br>    print(v)          # the tag prints each member whatever kind it is</code><br>The `for` lowering recognised a container *variable* as an iterable and not a container *literal*, so a literal reached `rangeBounds`' catch-all — `0 .. g.value(iterable)` — whose upper bound was the folded global. Marking a set/dict literal iterable as the runtime container it is, and recording the loop variable as interned text when every key is text, is the same rule the variable leg already applied. A generator call's result stays on its own lowering: routing every iterable through the container builder turned `for y in g(3)` into a refusal, which is a compiled leg losing a shape it used to answer. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-70) |
| Gap R.72 | A `;`-separated line is a program to `--interp`, a refusal to `--jit`/`--aot`, and a clean compile to `--emit-llvm` | ✅ `DONE` — decided 2026-10-02 with ADR 0242: `;` is a statement separator, so it is a token (`TokSemi`) and never a diagnostic; the empty statement moved to the parser, where every entry point has to agree with it | lexer/parser | 0240, 0242 | `pkg/lang/semicolon_test.go` — 13 programs × both engines vs CPython (inline suites included), `TestSemicolonIsNotADiagnostic`, five rejection rows each also rejected by the interpreter, and an indentation regression guard | `;` as a statement separator (CLOSED)<br><code>x = 5; print(x+1)   # every entry point: 6</code><br>Three entry points, one verdict now. The lexer had no case for `;`: it fell through the operator scan, `filterLex` turned the token into an error diagnostic and dropped it from the stream, the parser carried on parsing both statements, and then each path kept its own opinion — `JITWithOptions` fails on any error diagnostic, `Compile` collects diagnostics and returns the IR anyway, the interpreter never asks. ADR 0240 made the refusal legible; this decides it. Two details carried the change: the token has to stay **in** the stream, because an inline suite is a list of simple statements and dropping the separator printed `1 2 step` where CPython prints `1 step 2 step` (a wrong answer every engine agreed on); and the statement loop must skip newlines and separators together, or `x = 5;` hands `parseStmt` a NEWLINE to parse as a statement. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-72) |
| Gap R.73 | `min(a, b)` and `max(a, b)` were refused where CPython accepts the two-argument form: `print(min(1, 5), max(1, 5))` answered `min/max expects 1 argument` interpreted and `min expects one argument` compiled | 🟨 `PARTIAL` — the source-visible form is paid by ADR 0256; the runtime halves are Gaps R.107–R.110 | both | 0256 | measured on three engines before (CPython `1 5`, both backends refusing) and after (all three `1 5`); `pkg/lang/min_max_values_test.go` + `integration/min_max_values_test.go`, and `programs/min_max_values.gy` is `match` on all three legs | `print(min(1, 5), max(1, 5))` ✅ paid<br><code>print(min(1, 5), max(1, 5))   # CPython 1 5 · --interp 1 5 · --aot 1 5</code><br><code>print(min(2.5, 1), max(1, 2.5))   # CPython 1 2.5 · both backends 1 2.5 (was 1.0 2.5 compiled)</code><br><code>print(min("b", "a"))   # CPython a · both backends a (was the intern table's index)</code><br>Both engines took one iterable and nothing else, so the varargs form CPython documents was simply absent. It surfaced here because `max(xs[0], 5)` was the natural "use an element numerically in a fold" probe and both engines refused it for a reason that has nothing to do with tags. What ADR 0256 paid is the rule, not just the arity: **a fold returns the candidate it chose**, so an int winner keeps `int` printing and text is ordered by content. What it did not pay is a candidate whose kind the module cannot see — the reduction can compare those but cannot carry the winner's kind out, and answering them is the tagged value word's (Gap R.109), with the runtime container on the list side (R.107), on the text side (R.108) and across a call boundary (R.110). Gap R.71's runtime reduction belongs to the same helper. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-73) |
| Gap R.74 | `[{1, 2} for x in xs]` parses as *a list holding one set comprehension*: `parseDictOrSet` had a `for` branch after the closing brace (there for the call-argument form `len({x*x} for x in xs)`) that stole the **enclosing** list comprehension's clause, so both backends answered the wrong program in agreement — `[{"k": x} for x in [1, 2]]` printed one dict holding both entries, and `{1, 2} for x in y` was accepted where CPython raises `SyntaxError` | ✅ `DONE` — found 2026-10-02 by comparing comprehension rows against CPython; fixed with ADR 0244: a display stops at its brace, and may finish itself into a comprehension on a following `for` only when it is not an element inside a `[` | parser | 0244 | `pkg/lang/comprehension_brace_element_test.go` — `TestBraceDisplayDoesNotStealTheEnclosingFor` (AST shape, 10 rows, incl. the call-argument reading that must survive) and `TestMisParsedShapeIsNotAcceptedAnymore`; `integration/comprehension_brace_element_test.go` (21 programs × both engines vs CPython) | `[{1, 2} for x in [1]]` → `print(len(d[0]))` is `2`; `[{"k": x} for x in [1, 2]]` → `print(len(d))` is `2` (CLOSED, both engines)<br>The assertion is the AST, not the output: two engines agreeing on printed text can still be agreeing on the wrong *program*. | Gap R.76 | A comprehension over a **set or dict** whose slots hold more than one kind answers the machine word: `out = [x for x in sa]` over `{1, "a", None}` printed `[1, 0, 0]` and iterating a dict printed its interned *key indices* (`[0, 1]` where CPython prints `['a', 2]`) | ✅ `DONE` — answered rather than refused, 2026-10-02 with ADR 0245: the comprehension's loop variable binds the same `(payload, tag)` pair `for` binds (ADR 0185), so the element arrives as what it is on both engines; the list-only door that ADR 0244 briefly put in front of this is gone, because a refusal that also refuses `[k for k in d]` over a text-keyed dict is not honesty, it is coverage lost | codegen | 0185, 0188, 0241, 0244, 0245 | `TestComprehensionOverAMixedContainerTagsItsLoopVariable` (`pkg/lang/comprehension_brace_element_test.go`), `TestComprehensionOverAMixedContainerMatchesCPython` + `TestComprehensionOverAMixedContainerStillRefusesHonestly` (`integration/comprehension_brace_element_test.go`) | a comprehension over a mixed set or dict prints interned indices (OPEN, compiled)<br><code>sa = {1, "a", None}<br>out = [x for x in sa]<br>print(out)                # CPython [1, 'a', None] · compiled: [1, 0, 0]<br>d = {}; d["a"] = 1; d[2] = "b"<br>out = [k for k in d]<br>print(out)                # CPython ['a', 2] · compiled: [0, 1]</code><br>`for` over such a container binds its loop variable by tag (ADR 0241); the comprehension loop never caught up, and the guard that turned that into a refusal for lists asked only `mixedLists`. | Gap R.77 | A comprehension walked a **dict** at stride 1: it read slots 0 and 1 of a two-word entry and called them the two keys, so `[k for k in d]` over `{1: "x", 2: "y"}` — nothing mixed about it — answered `[1, 0]` where CPython answers `[1, 2]`, and `{1: "x", "k": 2}` answered `['k', 'x']` | ✅ `DONE` — closed 2026-10-02 with ADR 0245: iterating a dict scales its counter by 2 and measures the object in entries (`rt_dict_len`), the same two facts `for v in d:` already took from the same registries | codegen | 0188, 0245 | `TestDictComprehensionEntriesCarryTheirOwnKeysAndValues` (both packages) | a comprehension iterating a dict reads values as keys (OPEN, compiled)<br><code>d = {}; d[1] = "x"; d[2] = "y"<br>out = [k for k in d]<br>print(out)                # CPython [1, 2] · compiled: [1, 0]<br>d2 = {1: "x", "k": 2}<br>print([v for v in d2])     # CPython [1, 'k'] · compiled: ['k', 'x']</code><br>`for` scales the counter because a dict entry is a key word and a value word; the comprehension loop never learned the rule, so the second "key" it found was the first value. | Gap R.81 | A **set accepts an unhashable member**: `sa = set(); sa.add([1]); print(len(sa))` answers `1` where CPython raises `TypeError: unhashable type: 'list'` | ⏳ `OPEN` — measured 2026-10-02 alongside Gap R.80; both backends add the element and report a length. | Gap R.84 | An **ordering of two texts compares their interned indices**, so the answer depends on the order the program mentioned the spellings: `print(1 if "b" > "a" else 0)` prints `1` in CPython and in the interpreter and printed `0` compiled, and `a > b` on two text variables was answered by whichever assignment ran first | ✅ `DONE` — paid 2026-10-02 with ADR 0248: both comparison doors (the value path and the condition path, plus the string-operand refusal that used to fire in between) route an ordering of two provable texts to `rt_str_order`, which asks `strcmp` | codegen | 0173, 0224, 0248 | `pkg/lang/text_order_test.go` (16 rows × both engines: literals, variables, conditions, a value stored in a variable, a string-method receiver, a function parameter, `sorted`) + `integration/text_order_test.go` (the same programs against the pinned oracle) + `pkg/lang/slot_ordering_test.go` (slot rows) | ordering of two texts (CLOSED)<br><code>print(1 if "b" &gt; "a" else 0)   # CPython 1 · --interp 1 · --aot 0 before ADR 0248</code><br>Equality of interned texts is sound on the index, because interning is content-addressed (ADR 0173); ordering is the one thing that is not, because an index records arrival. `rt_sort` has carried a mode argument and compared elements with `strcmp` since ADR 0173 and the operators were never given the same question — so a program could sort a list of texts correctly and then compare two of its elements wrongly, which is the two-doors shape this ledger keeps hitting. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-84) |

All three now ask `slotTagSelfDescribing` through one `elemsTaggable`; `value()`'s literal gates ask `literalNeedsHeap(n) \|\| g.literalNeedsTags(n)`, because a bool's payload fitting an `i32` is exactly why the static global cannot hold it; the interpreter boxes a slot's verdict as a `kind:"bool"` object (the float box's shape, not a parallel array); `rt_payload_eq`'s numeric family is int, float **and** bool, so `True == 1`, `[True] == [1]`, `1 in {True}` and `{True, 1}` keep CPython's answers; and `numericSlotUse` keeps `xs[i] + 1` on a tagged bool slot answering `2` instead of becoming a refusal. What the tag does not reach is a boundary — Gap R.111 stays open, and the four residuals this row's measurement produced are Gaps R.116–R.119 below. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-112) |
| Gap R.113 | **`except <Type> as e:` does not parse**: `except ZeroDivisionError as err:` dies with `gustyc: parse error at 3:26: expected ":"` (and a follow-on `unexpected token` on the arm) at exit 1 on both paths, where CPython binds the exception and prints `division by zero` | ⏳ `OPEN` — measured 2026-10-02 while probing bools through a `try`; newly measured, so it has a row and no test yet | parser, both | — | none yet: the shape is written down here first, and the closure owes a program that runs on three engines plus the test that pins it today | `except Type:` dispatches in source order (ADR 0213) and a bare `except:` catches anything, but there is no way to *name* what was caught: the `as` clause is not in the grammar, so `err` cannot be printed, stored or inspected. `docs/language.md` never promised the binding, which makes this unimplemented surface rather than a regression — and the reason it is filed here is that a bool cycle reached for it and found nothing. | — |

| Gap R.133 | **The whole number past the compiled `int` word**: `print(floor(2147483648.0))`, `print(floor(-2147483649.0))` and `print(ceil(2147483647.1))` answer CPython's number in the evaluator and raise `OverflowError: floor: the whole number is beyond the word this backend's int holds (roadmap L12.12)` compiled (exit 3, catchable) | ⏳ `OPEN` — measured 2026-10-03 landing ADR 0264; owner: L12.12 | both | 0264 | `programs/probe_whole_number_beyond_the_int_word` in `conformance_cases.go` (interpreter pinned at `3000000000\n3000000000\n`, aot pinned `Missing`), `TestTheWholeNumberBuiltinsBeyondTheCompiledIntWordAreFiledNotSilent` (both sides, plus the catchability), and the boundary pair in `pkg/lang/math_names_test.go` | The compiled `int` is an `i32` and `fptosi double … to i32` outside the word is **poison, not a wrong number** — on x86-64 `cvttsd2si` answers `0x80000000` for any double too large, so `floor(3e9)` would print `-2147483648` with exit 0. `floorCeilValue` asks first (`fcmp uno`, `fcmp oge 2147483648.0`, `fcmp olt -2147483648.0`) and raises the reference's own class, naming the row that owns the decision; the evaluator's ints are `int64` and answer CPython's number, so the legs disagree and the disagreement is what the row records.<br>The boundary is the row's real content and it nearly shipped broken: the lower bound was first `-2147483649.0` with `fcmp olt`, reasoning "one past the minimum is the first value out" — but the i32 holds −2147483648 … 2147483647, so `-2147483649.0` is out of range, the test missed it, and it reached the poison `fptosi` and printed **`2147483647`**, a positive number from a negative input. Walking the edge found it; the middle of the range had nothing to say either way. Clamping to `2147483647` was rejected for the same reason wrapping was: a plausible wrong answer is the one no test catches, and Gap R.64 is the ledger's record of what silent overflow already costs. | [→](docs/roadmap-details.md#gap-r-133) |

The fix is in the emitter rather than at the call sites: the IEEE bit patterns (`0x7FF0000000000000`, `0xFFF0000000000000`, `0x7FF8000000000000`), which is what a double literal is made of anyway and free of the sign-loss the `fadd double 0.0, …` idiom carries (Gap R.132). Exit 1 is the right class only for a program the reference rejects. The workaround is `float("1e18")`, whose argument the conversion parses exactly. ## Definition of done per item

An item is `✅ DONE` when it ships:

- a unit test in `pkg/lang/` exercising the behaviour (and the failure mode — a stub that
  breaks the mechanism must make the test fail);
- an `integration/` whole-program compile-and-run case where applicable, asserting
  interpreter / AOT / JIT parity **and** the CPython verdict;
- a `docs/adr/` entry for any non-obvious decision (or a renumbering of an existing one);
- an update to `docs/language.md` and `docs/operations.md` when it changes user-visible
  syntax, flags, schemas or exit codes;
- a row in this file whose `Status`, `Evidence` and `Record` cells tell the truth —
  including the ledger row being *deleted* when the row was a debt.

## Definition of done for gap-shaped work

A gap is `✅ DONE` when the previously interpreter-only path also lowers on AOT (or the
both-backend parity program passes), no `interpreter-only` / `not lowered` comment remains
in `codegen.go` for it, and its pin is gone from `integration/conformance_cases.go`. A
`🟨 PARTIAL` row must name the remainder in its own cell, and that remainder appears once
in the [queue](#open-queue--the-working-list).

## Sequencing note

Gaps A–I are closed and Phases 3–10 are largely closed; the **current** work is Phase 11 —
the value model. L11.9 landed first (ADR 0186) because it is the harness that proves the
rest; L11.1 has taken its steps for lists, dicts and sets (ADRs 0182 / 0184 / 0185 / 0187 /
0189 / 0232), so the queue is now the **25 pinned `debt` rows**: floats and nested
containers inside elements, bools as values (L11.2), tuples (L11.3), code-point strings
(L11.5), numerics (L11.6), functions as values (L11.7), then the refusal diagnostics
(L11.8). L7.2 / L7.3 / L8.1 / L8.3 all read the tag — they follow L11.1. The still-open
gap-shaped items (Gap J.2, Gap K.8's frame stack, Gap M.2's default flip, Gap L.5, Gaps
N.2 / P.1 / P.2, Gaps Q.1 / Q.2 and the open `Gap R.*` rows) are absorbed by Phase 11 where
they are representation decisions and stay their own work where they are not; `--file`
flips to the compiled leg only once the corpus is green through it, which since L11.9 is a
measured claim rather than an assumption.

**Phase 12 sits beside Phase 11, not behind it.** The survey that produced L12.1 – L12.13
(2026-10-01, [record](docs/roadmap-details.md#phase-12)) sorted the Python-visible surface
into three states a construct is allowed to be in — implemented and CPython-equal, refused by
a code with a documented exit class, or absent from the manifest — and the eight silent wrong
answers are the queue's priority-1 class the same way an unwritten slot is. Three of its rows
are Phase 11's by root cause and are *not* started early: L12.9's `(null)` dict binding is
L11.1's tag, L12.3's renderer is L11.2's `str`/`repr` pair, L12.12's integer width is L11.1's
tagged word. The rest have no dependency on the representation and can be taken in any order,
and three of them are the cheapest work in the whole tracker relative to what they unlock —
class-body attributes (Gap R.56, one missing write), the keyword table (Gap R.59, four
statements), and the generated manifest (Gap R.65, which is how an agent stops guessing).
